> Consider scheduling a 3×3 convolution layer in ResNet50 [34] with 256 input and output channels, and an output dimension of  $14 \times 14$ , on an accelerator with five levels of memory. If we split each individual loop bound into its prime factors and assign each one to a memory level, we would have billions of schedules to consider. Among the randomly sampled schedules from all possible loop tilings, half of them fail to satisfy the buffer capacity constraints (e.g. a schedule is invalid if it requires a 4KB buffer, though the available buffer size is only 2KB.). Fig. 1 shows the performance distribution of the valid schedules. We observe a wide performance difference among the valid schedules, with the best one outperforming the worst one by  $7.2\times$ . In addition, we observe clusters of schedules that have similar latencies in the Fig. 1, revealing structure in the solution space.

> ## CoSA optimizes operator-level schedules for mapping DNN layers onto spatial DNN accelerators. Specifically, CoSA formulates the scheduling problem as a constrained-optimization
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20123.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> problem with *variables* representing the schedule, *constraints* representing DNN dimensions and hardware parameters, and objective functions representing goals, such as maximizing buffer utilization or achieving better parallelism. Fig. 2 shows the target problem space of CoSA. CoSA takes the specifications of the DNN layers and the underlying spatial accelerator as input constraints and generates a valid and high-performance schedule based on the objective functions in one pass.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20124.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> - 1) Target Workload: The work targets the DNN operators that can be expressed by a nested loop with 7 variables as loop bounds: R, S, P, Q, C, K, N. R and S refer to the convolution kernel width and height, P and Q refer to the output width and height, C refers to the input channel size, K refers to the output channel size, and N refers to the batch size, as illustrated in Fig. 2. The convolution operation computes the dot product of the filter size  $R \times S \times C$  of inputs and weights to generate one point in the output. Matrix multiplications can be expressed in this scheme as well.
> - 2) Target Architecture: CoSA targets spatial architectures with an array of processing elements (PEs) connected via an on-chip network and with multiple levels of memory hierarchy, a commonly adopted architecture template in today's DNN accelerator designs [18], [19], [28], [29], [36], [44], [54], [55], [60], [69], [71].
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20125.png)

schedule example

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> 3) Target Scheduling Decisions: CoSA-generated schedules describe how a specified DNN layer is executed on a given spatial architecture. Listing 1 shows an example of a schedule. Here, we use a loop-nest representation [49] to explicitly describe how the computation of a convolution layer is mapped to levels of memory hierarchies. We highlight three aspects of the schedule: 1) loop tiling, which describes which loops are mapped to which memory level and the values of the loop bounds; 2) **loop permutation**, which handles the relative ordering between loops in the same memory hierarchy; and 3) spatial mapping, which defines which loops are mapped to parallel spatial resources (shown as spatial\_for loops in Listing 1). All three factors play a key role in the efficiency of the scheduling choice. Next, we highlight the implications of loop permutation and spatial mapping, both of which are less explored than the well-studied loop tiling.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20126.png)

> for q2 = [0 : 2):
> // Global Buffer level
> for p2 = [0 : 7) :
>  for q1 = [0 : 7) :
>   for n0 = [0 : 3):
>    spatial\_for r0 = [0 : 3) :
>    spatial\_for k1 = [0 : 2) :

>     spatial\_for k0 = [0 : 2) :
>      // Weight Buffer level
>      for c1 = [0 : 2) :
>       for p1 = [0 : 2):
>        // Accumulation Buffer level
>        for s0 = [0 : 3) :
>        for p0 = [0 : 2):
>         spatial\_for c0 = [0 : 8) :
>          // Register
>          for q0 = [0 : 2):
> ```

Factors in s list are for spatial mapping, and factors in t list are for temporal mapping. For example, s:P4C4, t:K4 represents a mapping where a factor 4 of the P dimension and a factor 4 of the P dimension are mapped to spatial execution in a system with 16 PEs, leaving P is factor 4 to temporal mapping.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20130.png)

> Fig. 4 shows the impact of spatial mapping on DNN execution. We notice that there is a  $4.3 \times$  gap between best (rightmost) and worst (leftmost) schedules for the layer in consideration. The fundamental reason for the differences is the different communication traffic generated by different spatial mapping options. The best schedule, i.e., the rightmost schedule in the figure (s:P2C4K2, t:P2K2), is obtained when factors P=2, C=4, K=2 are mapped to the spatial loops, which cannot be achieved by simply choosing either model or data parallelism in the spatial partition. As a result, a systematic evaluation of different spatial mapping choices is required to find a good schedule.
> 
> The rest of the section discusses how CoSA formulates the scheduling variables, constraints, and objectives to solve the DNN scheduling problem.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20131.png)

定义 **schedule** configuration = prime factor allocation，用variable矩阵X表示调度；

factor（**n**）-dimension（**j**）allocation decision：memory level（**i**）、permutation order（**z**）、spatial Mapping（**k**）；

每个factor**仅使用一次**，即仅分配到一个memory level的slot；

factor分配到相同level memory中不同rank的slot，根据**rank大小**决定该factor-dimendion的次序；  

**相同dimension的不同factor**分配到相同rank的不同slot时，会**合并**到相同的factor-dimendion；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> 1) Variable Representation: We devise a mathematical representation for the DNN schedules and formulate the scheduling problem as a prime-factor allocation problem. Given a layer specification, we first factorize each loop bound into its *prime\_factors*. If the loop bound themselves are large prime number, we can pad them and then factorize. We assign each prime factor to a scheduling configuration that is composed of a combination of three decisions: 1) the mapped memory level, 2) the permutation order, and 3) the spatial mapping. Each prime factor has exactly one scheduling configuration.
> 
> Here, we use a binary matrix X to represent the prime factor allocation, i.e., the scheduling space, shown in Table III. The four dimensions of X are: 1) the layer dimension variables (indexed by j), 2) the prime factors of the loop bounds (indexed by n), 3) whether it is a spatial or temporal mapping (indexed by k), and 4) the memory and the permutation levels (indexed by i). With the prime factor decomposition, CoSA's encoding can represent all possible schedules and guarantees that the optimization solves for the full search space.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20132.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> the schedule shown in Listing 1. First, CoSA performs the tiling optimizations by assigning the prime factors to different memory levels. For example, dimension K is split into two tiles, where the inner tile of size 2 is allocated to the input buffer, and the outer tile of size 2 is allocated in the global buffer. Second, mapping a prime factor to *spatial* execution is indicated by whether the factor is mapped to a spatial column s or a temporal column t in the table. In this example, both prime factors for K are spatially mapped. Finally, for loop permutation, we add

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> 2) Constant Parameters: In addition to the loop-related variables, we have intrinsic relations across different components in the architecture and layer specifications which must be encoded by constant parameters. CoSA uses two constant binary matrices to encode the unique relations in the DNN scheduling space, shown in Tabel IV. The first binary constant matrix, A, encodes the association between layer dimensions (i.e., rows of the matrix) and data tensors (i.e., columns of the matrix). For each input (IA), weight (W), and output (OA) tensor, matrix A indicates which layer dimensions, i.e., R, S, P, Q, C, K, N, should be used to calculate the data transaction size as well as multicast and reduction traffic on the accelerators.
> 
> In addition, we introduce another binary matrix **B** to represent which memory hierarchy can be used to store which data tensor. DNN accelerators typically deploy a multi-level memory hierarchy, where each memory level can be used to store different types of data tensors. For example, matrix **B** shown in Table IV represents an architecture that has dedicated input and weight buffers for input activation and weight, respectively, while providing a shared global buffer to store input and output activations.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20137.png)

> We then set the upper bound of the buffer utilization to the capacity of different buffer sizes, represented using  $M_{I,v}$ . However, a problem with this utilization constraint is that it involves products of the decision variables X, making it nonlinear and infeasible to solve with standard constraint solvers. To address this limitation, we take the logarithm of both sides of the constraints to obtain a linear expression for the utilization and encode the if-else statement as:
> 
> $$U_{I,v} = \sum_{i=0}^{6, N_j} \sum_{j=0, n=0}^{1} \log(prime\_factor_{j,n}) A_{j,v} B_{I,v} X_{(j,n),i,k}$$
> 
> $$\leq \log(M_{I,v}), \forall I$$
> (2)
> 
> To encode different precisions for different data tensors, we add the logarithm of the datatype sizes  $precision_v$  to  $U_{I,v}$ .

！！！：B（I，v）在累乘计算U（I，v）过程中是定值！

**（j，n）**累乘是**不同dimension的不同factor累乘**，dimension质因数分解的逆过程；

**k**累乘是**dimension时间tiling或空间映射的factor累乘**，dimension按时间tiling的factor表示**串行#factor次tile**（记为k=1），dimension映射到空间的factor表示**#factor个tile并行**（记为k=0）；

**i**累乘是level-I memory的**I/W/O tiles**存储需求需要将分配到level-**0~I memory的factor累乘**，高层tile按时间tiling或空间映射到若干低层的sub-tile，对应程序的嵌套loop；

> 2) Spatial Resource Constraint: Another set of CoSA constraints is from the limited number of spatial resources. At the chip level, there is a limited number of PEs. At the PE level, there is a limited number of multiply-and-accumulate (MAC) units. In CoSA, once a factor is assigned to spatial mapping in the configuration, it needs to satisfy: 1) each problem factor can only be mapped to either spatial or temporal execution, 2) factors that map to spatial execution do not exceed the resource limit in the architecture. These two constraints can

目标函数——Utilization：提高Buffer使用率，达到更低的通信开销；

最大化**系统Util**，**Util**是每个存储层次**level-i**下**张量I、W、O**的**U（i，v）**求和；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> 1) Utilization-Driven Objective: High on-chip buffer utilization improves data-reuse opportunity. As demonstrated in the prior work [25], communication lower bounds can be achieved when the tiling block size is optimized for buffer utilization in a system with one-level cache. In this work, we formulate a utilization objective that aims to maximize the buffer utilization of all tensors, so the overall communication is minimized. We use the same formulation for the buffer utilization as in III-C1 and maximize the following linear utilization function:
> 
> $$\hat{Util} = \sum_{i=0}^{I-1} \sum_{v=0}^{2} U_{i,v}$$
>  (5)
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20144.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> Here, maximizing the sum of utilization for all buffer levels and all tensors in the logarithm form is equivalent to maximizing the geometric mean of the buffer utilization. Users can also attach weights to the different buffer levels or different data tensors if they want to optimize for the utilization of a specific level of the memory.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20145.png)

> 
> $$\leq \log(M_{I,v}), \forall I$$

目标函数——Traffic：每次传输的**数据量**、#spatial factor数量的**广播和单播**、#temporal factor数量的**迭代**；

data size per transfer：类似张量v在level-l的占用U（I，v），但**不需要B（I，v）**判断level-I是否存储张量v，计算**张量v在全部level memory的占用**，即将分配后张量v的dimension-factor重新收集；

？？？可以直接用张量demension计算，不需要从factor构建，和schedule无关？

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> 3) Traffic-Driven Objective: Communication latency is a key contributing factor to the performance of spatial architecture. CoSA also includes a traffic-driven objective to capture the communication cost. Specifically, communication traffic can be decomposed into three terms: 1) data size per transfer, 2) spatial factors of multicast and unicast traffic, and 3) temporal iterations. Multiplying these three factors will get the total amount of traffic in the network. Next, we discuss how we capture each of these factors using CoSA's representation.
> 
> First, similar to the buffer utilization expression, data size per transfer can computed using the allocated prime factors in matrix X, together with the dimension-tensor correlation matrix A, as shown in the equation below:
> 
> $$D_{v} = \sum_{i=0}^{I-1} \sum_{j=0, n=0}^{6, N_{j}} \sum_{k=0}^{1} \log(prime\_factor_{j,n}) A_{j,v} X_{(j,n),i,k}$$

> matrix A can be used to calculate different traffic patterns for different variables. For example, as shown in Fig. 5a, if the dimension P is mapped spatially,  $A_{P,W} = 0$  implies multicast traffic for weight tensor W. Since weight is not related to P, when we send weights from global buffer to PEs, the weight traffic will be multicasted to the destination PEs. If the dimension C is mapped spatially,  $A_{C,W} = 1$  (Fig. 5b) implies unicast traffic for weight tensor W as weight is related to C. Similarly, if the dimension C is mapped spatially,  $A_{C,OA} = 0$ (Fig. 5c) implies reduction traffic for output tensor OA, where partially sum needs to be reduced across C before sending back to GB. > ## A. Evaluation Platforms We evaluate the schedules generated by CoSA on two
> 
> platforms: 1) Timeloop for cycle performance and energy consumption, and 2) our cycle-exact NoC simulator for overall latency performance. The latter more accurately captures the communication overhead and concurrent hardware behaviors
> 
> Timeloop provides microarchitecture and technology-specific energy models for estimating the performance and energy on DNN accelerators. Timeloop reports the performance in terms of the maximum cycles required for each
> 
> processing element to complete the workload and to perform
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20158.png)

> by multiplying the access count on each hardware component with the energy per access and summing the products up. The access count is inferred from the schedule and the energy per access is provided by an energy reference table in Timeloop. **NoC Simulator** augments the Timeloop analytical compute model for PEs with a synthesizable NoC implementation to reflect the communication cost. Communication is one of the key contributing factors for latency in a NoC-based system, especially for the communication bound schedules. The NoC simulator is transaction-based and cycle-exact for modeling the on-chip traffic. Leveraging the synthesizable SystemC router design from Matchlib [41] that supports
> 
> memory accesses, assuming perfect latency hiding with double
> 
> buffering. The energy consumption in Timeloop is calculated
> 
> D mesh network and implement an X-Y routing scheme. The simulator captures both computation and communication latencies by concurrently modeling data transfers in the NoC, the PE executions, and off-chip DRAM accesses based on the DRAMSim2 model [58], where the impact of traffic congestion on the NoC can also be manifested.
>

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ## B. Baseline Schedulers
> 
> We evaluate CoSA with respect to two other scheduling schemes: 1) a Random scheduler that searches for five different valid schedules, from which we choose the one with the best result for the target metric, and 2) the **Timeloop Hybrid** mapper in Timeloop [49] that randomly selects a tiling factorization, prunes superfluous permutations, and then linearly explores the pruned subspace of mappings before it proceeds to the next random factorization. For this mapper, we keep the default termination condition where each thread self-terminates after visiting 500 consecutive mappings that are valid yet sub-optimal. The mapper is run with 32 threads, each of which independently searches the scheduling space until its termination condition is met. Once all threads have terminated, Timeloop returns the best schedule obtained from all 16,000+ valid schedules.
> 
> ## C. Experiment Setup
> 
> Gurobi [32], a general-purpose optimization solver for MIP and other constrained programming, as the solver. We specify the CoSA variables, constraints, and objective functions before we invoke the solver. The solver takes at most seconds to return a schedule for DNN layers.
>

The precision used for the benchmarks is 8-bit for the input and weights, and 24-bit for the partial sums. We do not pad the dimensions to be multiples of 2, as it incurs more overhead and outweighs the benefits it provides to allow more scheduling options. **Baseline architecture:** We consider a spatial-array architecture like Simba [59] as our baseline. # TC24:TensorMap

**TC24**:TensorMap: A Deep RL-Based Tensor Mapping Framework for Spatial Accelerators

## idea

tensor Mapping背景

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ## II. BACKGROUND
> 
> ## A. Mapping for Tensor Computation
> 
> We provide an example of mapping a 1D convolution onto spatial accelerators. Given weight tensor W[2], input tensor I[6], and output tensor O[5], the 1D convolution can be represented as a loop nest:
> 
> ```
> for (x=0; x<6-2+1; x++) {
>   for (k=0; k<2; k++) {
>     O[x] += I[x+k] * W[k];
>   }
> }</pre>
> ```
> 
> In this loop, there exist no unrolling loops, the tiling size of each loop is 1, and the ordering is from the input tensor to the weight tensor (denoted as  $I \to W$ ).
> 
> To improve parallelism and data reuse, the loop nest can be rewritten. First, we can interchange the loops from  $I \to W$  to  $W \to I$  as follows:
> 
> ```
> for (k=0; k<2; k++) {
>   for (x=0; x<6-2+1; x++) {
>     O[x] += I[x+k] * W[k];
>   }
> }</pre>
> ```
> 
> Then, we can unroll the weight loop using multiple PEs of spatial accelerators as shown here:
> 
> ```
> parallel_for (k=0; k<2; k++) {
>   for (x=0; x<6-2+1; x++) {
>     O[x] += I[x+k] * W[k];
>   }
> }</pre>
> ```
> 
> Finally, we can add loops by changing the tiling size to exploit data reuse. This loop nest is rewritten as follows:
> 
> ```
> parallel_for (k=0; k<2; k++) {
>     for (x1=0; x1<6/2; x1++) {
>         for (x0=0; x0<2; x0++) {
>             x = x1*2 + x0;
>             O[x] += I[x+k] * W[k];
>         }
>     }
> }</pre>
> ```
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20162.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**

> an outer loop over the tiled loop. Fig. 3 shows the process of mapping 1D convolution onto a spatial accelerator. The weight tensor is spatially distributed across two PEs. Each weight of W[0] and W[1] is multicast across time, and each input of I[1], I[3] and I[5] is multicast across PEs. This mapping configuration exploits parallelism of weight loop, spatial reuse of input data, and temporal reuse of weight data. Similarly, the mapping of a 2D convolution onto accelerators can be achieved with the NVDLA mapping configuration shown in Fig. 1(c).
> 
> In this case, we set the tiling size of the input loop to 2, and add
> 
> ## The sub-space of each mapping primitive is described as: • $M_0 = \mathbb{Z}$ : the level of unrolling.
> 
> B. Mapping Space
> 
> - M<sub>1</sub> = Z: the unrolling loop at each level.
>   M<sub>2</sub> = Z<sup>d</sup>: the tiling size of each loop at each level, where
> - d is the number of loops. •  $M_3 = \mathbb{Z}$ : the loop ordering at each level.
> 
> Given a spatial accelerator A and a targe tensor computation T, the mapping space is defined as:
> 
>  $\mathbf{M_{A,T}} = \{ map \in (M_1 \times M_2 \times M_3)^{M_0} | 0 < H(map) \le H^* \}$  where H(map) represents the required hardware resources when using the mapping configuration map, and  $H^*$  represents the available hardware resources of the accelerator.
> 
> pends on both the target tensor computation and the available hardware resources of the spatial accelerator. The tensor computation impacts on the maximum of  $M_0$ ,  $M_1$ ,  $M_2$ , and  $M_3$ , while the accelerator determines the number of valid mapping configurations in the search space. For example, the dimension

**搜索方法**问题：暴力开销大而难求最优解、模拟退火和遗传是黑盒且陷入局部最优解、DL方法没有考虑不同Acc架构；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ## A. Disadvantages of Existing Search-Based Works
> 
> Exhaustive Search. Previous efforts such as Timeloop [22], dMazeRunner [25], and Marvel [24] first prune the mapping search space with heuristics. For example, they limit the tiling size and fix the ordering of specific loops. Then they use an exhaustive search to find the globally optimal mapping configuration in the pruned search space. Regrettably, this approach is expensive. It requires domain expert knowledge to design heuristics and intensive time to evaluate all the mapping configurations in the pruned space.
> 
> **Black-Box Optimization.** Previous efforts [15], [16], [18], [20] leverage black-box optimization approaches to perform mapping space search. AutoTVM [18] relies on simulated annealing, FlexTensor [15] leverages Q-learning, and both Ansor [16] and AMOS [20] employ genetic algorithms. These approaches concurrently determine each mapping primitive in the large search space and neglect the relationship between primitives. The relationship is that the tiling size is constrained by available hardware resources and impacted by loop unrolling.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20165.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> Excessive tiling can violate resource constraints, while insufficient tiling can lead to low resource utilization and decrease data reuse. Furthermore, excessive tiling of unrolling loops can decrease parallelism. These approaches depend on massive simulations to find optimized solutions, which decreases productivity and flexibility for diverse tensor computations. After only a few searching steps, they easily converge towards local optima.
> 
> **Deep Learning.** Recent Mind Mappings [21] leverages deep learning algorithms to train a differentiable surrogate for the cost function. In contrast to the cost function f in Equation 2, the surrogate facilitates a greater number of searching steps per unit time. Mind Mappings collects a large-scale dataset to train the surrogate on the specific accelerator. Regrettably, the surrogate can adapt to different tensor computations but cannot adapt to different accelerators.
> 
> Moreover, a majority of these frameworks perform mapping space search with fixed 2-level loop unrolling. Consequently, it is non-trivial for them to support higher-level unrolling to exploit more parallelism.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20166.png)

RL的**优势**是：

1、序列优化而容易找到较优解；

2、可微分可训练而应用更多算法和Acc架构（**泛化？但重点是抽象不同架构的特征引入训练？**）；

3、不用人工构建Mapping的搜索过程而使用agent（Mapping**建模**依赖人工假设）

RL的挑战是：

1、Action空间**离散且动态变化**，policy网络如何从分布广泛的空间中决策Action；

2、稀疏的Reward，序列化优化需要做出一系列Action才能评估性能来计算Reward，每一步需要设置dense reward（设置初始配置，之后每一步改变后都是**完整**配置）；

3、最优解和可用性：巨大搜索空间收敛到最优解较慢，增大搜索粒度更快但不一定最优解（RL粗筛+GA精细优化）；

> ## B. Advantages of Reinforcement Learning
> 
> Mapping space search requires multiple decisions in nature, including loop unrolling, tiling, and ordering. This motivates us to formulate mapping space search as a multi-step decision-making problem, which can be solved through RL.
> 
> **Sequential Modeling.** As a sequential decision process, the RL agent can leverage insights from preceding decisions to inform subsequent ones. Specifically, depending on the primitives determined at previous time steps, the agent can determine one primitive at the current time step to improve parallelism and data reuse. Thus RL can exploit the relationship between mapping primitives and generate effective decision sequences. **Differentiability.** The agent can perform gradient-based
> 
> the guidance of gradient, the agent can rapidly converge to optimized mapping configurations after a few searching steps. Thus the agent can boost productivity and flexibility for diverse tensor computations and spatial accelerators with the efficient search process.
> 
> Design Ease. RL makes designers focus more on what to
> 
> search through RL training algorithms [26], [27], [28]. With
> 
> optimize by defining cost function. Prior works require hand-tuned heuristics to facilitate the mapping space search process. Instead, the agent can automatically derive such heuristics by using deep neural networks as the policy network. In this way, integrating RL into the mapping space search can significantly ease the designers' burden.

> ## V. TENSORMAP
> 
> ## A. Overview
> 
> Fig. 4 shows the workflow of TensorMap, which comprises RL-based mapping search and GA-based refinement. TensorMap takes tensor computations as input and generates optimized mapping configurations. The tensor computations are implemented by deep learning frameworks, and the mapping configurations are applied to spatial accelerators.
> 
> The RL-based search contains 7 \* U time steps, which is regarded as an episode, where U indicates the level of unrolling. At the  $t^{th}$  time step, the agent interacts with the environment as follows:
> 
> - The agent observes state  $S_t$  from environment and takes  $S_t$  as the input for its policy network  $\pi_{\theta}$ . The state  $S_t$  is described in the table of Fig. 5(b) and 5(c).
> - The agent generates a probability distribution  $a_t$   $(a_t \sim \pi_{\theta}(a_t|S_t))$  over the action space through its policy network  $\pi_{\theta}$ . The action  $A_t$ , which represents the unrolling loop or the tiling size of one loop, is then chosen based on this distribution.
> - The environment receives  $A_t$  and provides both a reward  $R_t$  and the next state to the agent.
> 
> After completing N episodes, the policy network is updated using the optimization function  $\mathbf{L}_{Map}$  to maximize the expected cumulative rewards at all time steps. After some update steps, the agent can use the optimized policy network to find a coarse-grained mapping configuration for the target tensor computation. Fig. 5(b) shows an example of 2-level unrolling.

> ping configuration obtained from the RL-based search. At each generation, new individuals of mapping configuration are produced through mutation and crossover, as depicted in Fig. 5(d). Well-performing individuals are selected into the next generation according to the ranking of their fitness in the environment. After completing G generations, a fine-grained mapping configuration is found and can be configured on the

> 
> spatial accelerator.
> 
> primitives in a sequential mode. It first determines the loop unrolling and then determines the tiling size of each loop. TensorMap uses its policy network with multiple output branches to generate different policies following the above decision order at each time step. It incorporates a *Bernoulli* distribution to automatically control the growth of the level of unrolling to support multi-level unrolling. In this way, TensorMap can address the inconsistent and dynamic action space. In the interactive environment, TensorMap builds an implicit mapping configuration.
>

> 
> and is updated at each time step. In this way, the agent can
> 
> receive an immediate reward from environment once taking an action. In the RL-based phase, TensorMap constructs a coarse-grained action space to make the original space more discrete. It leverages RL to find reasonably good coarse-grained mapping configurations after a few steps. In the GA-based refinement phase, TensorMap leverages GA to refine coarse-grained configurations and obtain near-optimal fine-grained mapping configurations after a few simulations. In this way, the two-phase TensorMap seeks to strike a balance between tractability and optimality.

> ② Training Algorithm. The training algorithm is used to update the parameters of the policy network by calculating approximate gradients of the optimization function  $L_{map}$ . Here REINFORCE [26] is selected as the training algorithm due to its computational efficiency. Given the policy  $a_t \sim \pi_{\theta}(a_t|S_t)$  and reward  $R_t$ , the optimization function  $L_{map}$  is calculated as follows:
> 
> $$\mathbf{L}_{Map} = -\frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^{N} \left[ \log \pi_{\theta}(a|S) \sum_{t=1}^{7*U} \gamma^{t} * R_{t} \right], \quad (3)$$
>  where  $N$  is the number of sampled episodes, and  $\gamma$  is the
> 
> are calculated as follows:  $\nabla_{\theta} \mathbf{L}_{map} = -\frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^{N} \left[ \nabla_{\theta} \log \pi_{\theta}(a|S) \sum_{t=1}^{7*U} \gamma^{t} * R_{t} \right] \quad (4)$ 
> 
> discounted factor. Then the approximate gradients  $\nabla_{\theta} \mathbf{L}_{map}$ 
> 
>  $\theta \leftarrow \theta - \alpha \sum_{t} \nabla_{\theta} \mathbf{L}_{map},$ 
> 
> where  $\alpha$  is the step size. In this way,  $\mathbf{L}_{map}$  can directly optimize the policy network by maximizing the expected cumulative discounted rewards. We set N to 10 and  $\gamma$  to 0.9 empirically. We use Adam optimizer to update the parameters, where  $\alpha$  is set to  $5e^{-4}$ .
> 
> 3 Action. The agent makes 7 actions at each level of
> 
> unrolling, as shown in Fig. 5(b). At the first time step, the agent determines the specific unrolling loop, which is chosen from dimensions K, C, Y, and X. At the remaining time steps, the agent determines the specific tiling size of one loop. The computation order is KCYXRS similar to the NVDLA mapping configuration. Since the tiling size of each loop ranges from 1 to the size of the corresponding dimension, we construct a coarse-grained action space:  $[2^0, 2^1, 2^1 + 2^0, 2^2, 2^2 + 2^1, ..., MTS]$ , to avoid the exploration of inefficient mapping configurations. Here MTS is the maximal tiling size of each loop. Meanwhile, this action space can reduce the opportunity of exceeding resource constraints.
> 
> 4 Implicit Mapping. The environment is equipped with
> 
> an implicit mapping, target tensor computation, resource constraints, and a reward function. The implicit mapping is constructed by initializing tiling and unrolling in Fig. 5(a). It is described in a table with U rows and 7 columns in Fig. 5(b). Each row represents a level of unrolling. The first column shows unrolling loops and the size of PE clustering, and the remaining columns show the tiling size of each loop. The implicit mapping records the RL agent's actions across time steps. Given an action from the agent, one element of the implicit mapping is updated, as shown in Fig. 5(b) and 5(c). Meanwhile, the environment uses the implicit mapping to generate immediate rewards

> ## B. RL-Based Mapping Search
> 
> ① **Policy Network.** Fig. 6 shows the composition of the policy network. The policy network encodes the input state as a hidden vector through fully connected layers (FC). It employs ReLU activation to enhance the nonlinearity in the Encoding MLP. Then the policy network uses Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) cells to memorize previous actions and avoid exceeding resource constraints. Finally, it utilizes FC and Softmax layer in the Decoding MLP to generate a policy. This policy represents a probability distribution over the action space. Notably, the Encoding MLP and LSTM cells share parameters across all time steps. To address the challenge of an inconsistent action space, one trivial solution is to ensure that the output size of the policy network is large enough to cover the sub-space of each mapping primitive. Subsequently, a masking mechanism is employed to limit invalid actions. Regrettably, it is difficult for this solution to converge to optimized mappings due to the diverse distribution of each sub-space. To facilitate mapping exploration, the policy network features multiple output branches. These branches are implemented by designing distinct FC layers in the Decoding MLP. In each branch, the shape of FC layer corresponds to the action space of loop unrolling or tiling. The
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20176.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> for the agent. It provides statistics such as delay and energy, and then transforms them into rewards through the reward function.
> 
> ⑤ State. The state is designed to reflect the mapping configuration and is updated with the implicit mapping. At the  $t^{th}$  time step, the state  $S_t$  is expressed as a 7 \* U dimensional vector:
> 
> $$S_t = [\{UD_i, KTS_i, CTS_i, YTS_i, XTS_i, RTS_i, RTS_i, STS_i\}_{i=1}^U]$$
> 
> $$S_{t+1} = F(S_t, A_t), \qquad (6)$$
> 
> where  $UD_i$  represents the unrolling loop, and other notations represent the tiling sizes of 6 loops at the  $i^{th}$  level of unrolling. In this way, once the agent makes an action, the environment provides the next state and a reward for it. Similar to the implicit mapping, only one element of the state is updated at each time step. The next state  $S_{t+1}$  is produced by modifying the current state  $S_t$  with a specific action  $A_t$ . In terms of the initial state  $S_0$ , the loops of unrolling are C and Y, and the tiling size of each loop is 1 as shown in Fig. 5(a).
> 
> **6 Reward Function.** As the feedback of environment, the reward is vital to guide the agent's policy. When the agent learns from trial and error, it aims to ensure that the cost exhibits an overall descent trend while the cumulative reward shows an overall ascent trend within an episode. To obtain this goal, the reward function is defined as:
> 
> $$R_{t} = \begin{cases} E_{t-1} - E_{t}, & H(map) \leq H^{*}, \\ -E_{max} * \beta, & H(map) > H^{*}, \end{cases}$$
> (7)
> 
> where  $E_t$  represents the cost of the accelerator when using the mapping configuration map at the  $t^{th}$  time step, and  $E_{max}$  is the maximum cost during all episodes. If the mapping configuration meets resource constraints, a positive  $R_t$  indicates that the agent's action is profitable, while a negative  $R_t$  is used to penalize the agent. This mechanism can help the agent learn from trial and error steadily. When violating platform constraints, the penalty factor  $\beta$  is set to 2 empirically.

## 实验

**tensor-stationary** mapping：OS、WS、IS；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ## VI. EVALUATION
> 
> ## A. Experiment Setup
> 
> Benchmarks. We evaluate TensorMap over a wide range of tensor workloads with diverse tensor computations. We choose five representative workloads: ResNet50 [4], UNet [7], MobileNet-V2 [33], BERT [34], and GPT [35]. ResNet50 mainly compose 2D convolution layers. It gradually reduces the resolution of input activation and deepens the channel of feature map for image classification. UNet consists of 2D convolution layers and transposed convolution layers. The former is used to reduce the resolution and deepen the channel. The latter is used to restore the original resolution and channel. UNet is designed for semantic segmentation, especially for biomedical imaging. MobileNet-V2 consists of 2D convolution layers and depthwise separable convolution layers. It is a lightweight model, specifically tailored for IoT devices. BERT and GPT are language models, consisting of self-attention layers. They are widely used for natural language processing applications such as machine translation and dialog systems.
> 
> to support a wide variety of spatial accelerators. We consider several representative accelerators: TPU [1] and Eyeriss [2]. TPU has 24 MB of global buffer and 65536 PEs. Each PE has 64 KB of local buffer. Eyeriss has 128 KB of global buffer and 256 PEs. Each PE has 4 KB of local buffer. We also evaluate TensorMap on a specifical accelerator in Section VI-D and on a FPGA-based accelerator in Section VI-E.
> 
> Target Spatial Accelerators. Our TensorMap is flexible
> 
> **Baseline Approaches.** We first compare TensorMap with tensor-stationary mappings, which includes weight-stationary, input-stationary and output-stationary configurations. Then we conduct a comprehensive comparison with popular mapping frameworks, including AutoTVM [18], FlexTensor [15], Ansor
> 
> [16], AMOS [20], and Mind Mappings [21]. These frameworks
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20178.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> We implement each framework using the provided source code. For a fair comparison, all approaches are run for a fixed number of searching steps or a fixed wall-clock time.
> 
> employ black-box optimization approaches outlined in Table I.
> 
> Hyperparameters. We keep all hyperparameters unchanged and re-perform TensorMap to adapt to diverse tensor computations. The hyperparameters including hidden size, learning rate, and parameter optimizer are set by referring to prior works [29], [31]. The hyperparameters including training algorithm, maximum level of unrolling, penalty factor, and discount factor are determined empirically.
> 
> Implementation. We implement TensorMap with the deep learning toolbox, Pytorch [12]. We leverage MAESTRO [36] to build the simulated environment in RL. MAESTRO is an analytical cost model for spatial accelerators and is widely used in related works [19], [24]. It takes the tensor computation, architecture configuration and mapping configuration as inputs and reports delay, energy, power, and area of the accelerator.

# ICCD24：TileMap

**ICCD24**:TileMap: Mapping Multi-Head Attention on Spatial Accelerators with Tile-based Analysis

算子融合MHA的Mapping，GEMM和CONV的Mapping扩展；

## idea

MHA的**计算强度**远低于Acc的性能期望，中间结果传输开销可优化；

**算子融合**让中间结果留在片上继续计算，降低传输开销；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> operational intensity and becomes memory bandwidth-bound. Operational intensity is defined as the number of compute operations divided by the number of memory accesses. For the first BMM, the number of operations is  $\mathcal{O}(BHLD^2)$ . The number of memory accesses for K, Q, and A is  $\mathcal{O}(BHLD + BHL^2)$ . Therefore, the operational intensity is  $\mathcal{O}(\frac{LD}{D+L})$ . Similarly, the operational intensity for the second BMM is also  $\mathcal{O}(\frac{LD}{D+L})$ . For example, NVDLA [9] has 5.6 TOPs/sec of int8 computation peak performance and 20 GB/s
> 
> tensors. As a result, the multi-head attention exhibits poor
> 
> TOPs/sec of int8 computation peak performance and 20 GB/s memory bandwidth. It requires an operational intensity of 280 FLOPS/B to prevent bandwidth bottlenecks. It is non-trivial to realize this operational intensity for multi-head attention. Thus the compute cores are underutilized due to memory bound.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20180.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> loops, which are typically equal. The first BMM computes the relation between K and Q, resulting in an attention score tensor A with size  $[B, H, L_1, L_2]$ . The Softmax operator normalizes each row of tensor A along the last dimension. The second BMM uses the normalized attention scores as a weight tensor and computes the weighted sum of V rows. It results in an output tensor O with size [B, H, L, D].
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20181.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> Despite its enormous potential, multi-head attention leads to high cost (e.g., memory access) when deploying transformer models [6]–[8]. There are two types of tensor operators in the multi-head attention: batch matrix multiplication (BMM) and Softmax, as shown in Fig. 1(a). These operators are offloaded on spatial accelerators (e.g., NVDLA [9]) with a memory hierarchy and massive parallel compute cores. From the memory perspective, each operator reads input tensors (e.g., K, Q, V, A, and A') from off-chip memory, transfers these tensors to on-chip memory, computes at the cores, and writes the resulting tensors (e.g., A, A', and O) back to offchip memory. As a result, the unnecessary off-chip memory accesses for intermediate tensors decrease the operational intensity of multi-head attention and lead to a performance bottleneck. This bottleneck becomes increasingly prominent with the evolution of hardware accelerators.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20182.png)

MHA算子融合的挑战：

1、MHA的**算子依赖约束**算子之间的**tile shape**相匹配；

2、每个层次下，**不同BMM**的数据**访问**方式可能不同，如K**不**tiling、V按**列**tiling、Q按**行**tiling，可能需要**off-chip重排或on-chip缓存等**数据route； 

3、Acc**资源约束**tiling；

> Fig. 1. (a) Tensor operators in multi-head attention. (b) The operator-fusion mapping flow for attention.

> compilers/mappers [10], [11]. It combines multiple operators into one single kernel and stages intermediate tensors in onchip memory. However, designing operator-fusion mapping for multi-head attention remains a challenging task for the following reasons. First, there exists data dependency between BMMs and Softmax, as shown in the shadow part of Fig. 1(b). The tile shape of the upstream BMM requires to match that of the downstream Softmax and BMM at a certain memory level (e.g., shared memory of NVDLA). Furthermore, each BMM may require diverse memory access patterns across the memory hierarchy. Existing mappers mainly fuse many-to-many operators (e.g., convolution) with one-to-one operators (e.g., ReLU). The data dependency and access diversity make these mappers unprofitable for multi-head attention. At last, the size of the intermediate tensors A and A' grows quadratically with sequence length. Spatial accelerators have limited hardware resources (e.g., on-chip memory). An operator-fusion mapping requires a careful selection of tile factors under resource constraints. Existing mappers incur high searching costs to find a high-performance mapping for multi-head attention.
> 
> To decrease memory access traffic and improve data reuse,
> 
> operator fusion is prevalent in conventional deep learning

Thus the output tile shape in shared memory is [8, 256, 64]. Unfortunately, Softmax is a many-to-many operator, requiring a tile factor for dimension  $L_2$  of 1 in global buffer and a tile shape of [H0,  $L_1$ 0, 512] in shared memory. Here H0 and  $L_10$  can be tunable shown in Fig. 2. For the second BMM, optimized tile factors for dimensions H,  $L_1$ , and  $L_2$  are 1, 16, and 4 respectively in global memory. Thus the input tile shape in shared memory is [16, 256, 64]. The tile factors for dimensions H and  $L_1$ are inconsistent between BMM operators. In brief, the data dependency and memory access diversity between operators make it difficult to reuse tiles in on-chip memory.

## 方法

基于tile分析，约束M-S-M间的**tile shape**，估计计算和存储**资源消耗**；

>建立**MHA算子融合**后的程序设计空间；

>**RL**搜索；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> design. First, we propose a tile-based analysis to construct a mapping design space. Within this space, each mapping candidate can satisfy data dependency and resource constraints. In detail, we summarize multiple rules on factors that are related to data dependency and resource constraints. We then apply such rules to limit the range of tunable factors. To facilitate mapping space exploration, we exploit reinforcement learning to implement a searching algorithm. The RL agent regards the mapping space as its action space, setting the probability of any invalid choice to 0. Over subsequent time steps, the agent further optimizes the action space to preserve all constraints. We evaluate TileMap using diverse multi-head
> 
> TileMap incorporates these insights into a novel mapper
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20187.png)

构建MHA**算子融合**的设计空间，on-chip代码是融合后tile计算，off-chip代码是MHA的**融合tiling**（inter-op tiling）：

1、**对齐**片上BMM-SFM-BMM的tile shape，直接复用片上的中间结果，对应M-S-M的**统一最外层for-loop**；

2、shared mem层确定tile shape后（即1），每个算子在**内层**存储上**独立优化**数据访问，对应**第二层各自的for-loop**；

3、估计数据占据的存储空间；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ```
> 1 // Off-chip memory
>   for b1, h1, l_11 in grid(B1, H1, L_11):
>     // On-chip memory
> 3
>     for b0, h0, l_10, l_2, d in grid(B0, H0, L_10, L_2, D):
>      b = b1*B0+b0, h = h1*H0+h0, ...
>      A[b, h, l_1, l_2] +=
>        Q[b, h, l_1, d] * K[b, h, d, l_2]
>     for b0, h0, l_10, l_2 in grid(B0, H0, L_10, L_2):
>      b = b1*B0+b0, h = h1*H0+h0, ...
>      A'[b, h, l_1, l_2] = Softmax(A[b, h, l_1, l_2])
> 10
>     for b0, h0, l_10, l_2, d in grid(B0, H0, L_10, L_2, D):

> 12
>       O[b, h, l_1, d] +=
> 13
>        A'[b, h, l_1, l_2] * V[b, h, l_2, d]
> 14
> ```

![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20189.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> TileMap aligns intermediate tiles across BMMs and Softmax and reuses them in a certain memory, such as the shared memory of NVDLA.
> 
> Tile alignment is realized through the following rules. 1) The dimensions  $L_2$  and D are not tiled in off-chip memory as shown in Fig. 1(b), since both BMM and Softmax are many-tomany operators. In this way, on-chip memory stages a block of rows of tensor A, and Softmax performs normalization on each row. 2) The tile factors B1, H1, and  $L_11$  for each operator in off-chip memory are kept the same. This ensures that the output tile shape of the upstream BMM can automatically match the input tile shape of the downstream Softmax and
> 
> BMM. These rules are specified in Fig. 2.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20190.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> the input tile shape of the downstream Softmax and BMM. As shown in Fig. 2, this shape is  $[B0, H0, L_10, L_2]$ . Here  $B1 = \frac{B}{B0}$ ,  $H1 = \frac{H}{H0}$ , and  $L_11 = \frac{L_1}{L_10}$  are tile factors that are no larger than the dimension sizes  $\overline{B}$ , H, and  $L_1$  respectively. In this way, these operators can be fused by reusing the intermediate tile in on-chip memory. Second, we observe that the optimization of memory access pattern for each operator is inherently independent at a lower memory level. After the tile shape is specified at the shared memory of NVDLA, the tensor can be further tiled and transferred to registers. In this case, the tile factors for each BMM are determined independently. Finally, the input/output tile sizes depend on tile factors. We can pre-calculate the maximal input/output tiles under resource constraints to ensure that each candidate is valid. This significantly decreases searching costs by avoiding the exploration of invalid mappings.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20191.png)

**Mapping空间**

inter-op tiling优化参数是off-chip tile factor：B1、H1、L1-1；

intra-op tiling是算子内tiling优化：B0、H0、L1-0、L2和D；

intra-op parallel是算子内的**spatial**数据流优化；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ![](_page_0_Figure_0.jpeg)
> 
> Fig. 4. Map BMM-Softmax-BMM on an abstracted accelerator with a three-level memory hierarchy.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20192.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> memory. In terms of NVDLA, L2, L1, and L0 represent global memory, shared memory, and register respectively. Input tiles flow towards lower-level memory, while output tiles flow towards successor operators and higher-level memory.
> 
> Based on tile alignment, the mapping space is expressed by
> 
> three primitives: inter-operator tiling, intra-operator tiling, and intra-operator parallelization. The inter-operator tiling involves choosing efficient tile factors in off-chip memory, including  $B1, H1, L_11$ . The intra-operator tiling is independent between operators, aiming to find optimized tile factors across the hierarchy. Specifically, we further split loops B0, H0,  $L_10$ ,  $L_2$ , and D in shared memory and transfer data to registers. The intra-operator parallelization is to parallelize loops using compute cores and select proper parallel factors. We observe that most mappings in the space are invalid to meet resource constraints of spatial accelerators. TileMap pre-calculates the maximal tile/parallel factors to ensure that each mapping
> 
> candidate is valid.

**资源约束**

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> Table I presents three mapping primitives along with their corresponding constraints. To begin with, both inter-operator and intra-operator tile factors are constrained by the on-chip memory capacity. On an abstracted accelerator, input/weight/output tensors are stored in the memory hierarchy when performing computation. Therefore, the required memory capacity can be formulated as:  $r_1(T^{er}) = S_1^I + S_1^W + S_1^O$ 
> 
> $$r_2(T^{ra}) = S_2^I + S_2^W + S_2^O$$
>  where  $S_1^I$ ,  $S_1^W$ , and  $S_1^O$  represent the sizes of input, weight,
> 
> and output tensors respectively using  $T^{er}$ , and  $S_2^I$ ,  $S_2^W$ , and  $S_2^O$  are tensor sizes using  $T^{ra}$ . On a specific accelerator, different memory stores different tensors. For instance, the shared memory of NVDLA stores input/weight tensors, while the register stores output tensor. Consequently, the required memory capacity  $r_1(T^{er})$  on NVDLA is rewritten as:
> 
> $$r_1(T^{er}) = S_1^I + S_1^W$$
>  in shared memory  $r_1(T^{er}) = S_1^O$  in register
> 
>  $S_1^I$ ,  $S_1^W$ , and  $S_1^O$  are calculated by:  $S_1^I = B0 * H0 * L_10 * D$ ,
> 
>  $S_1^W = B0 * H0 * D * L2$ , and  $S_1^O = B0 * H0 * L_10 * L2$ , where B0, H0, and  $L_10$  are inter-operator tile factors shown in Fig. 2. When running the second BMM, A', V and O are input, weight, and output tensors respectively.  $S_1^I$ ,  $S_1^W$ , and
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20194.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> $S_1^O$  are calculated by:  $S_1^I = B0 * H0 * L_10 * L2$ ,  $S_1^W = B0 * H0 * L2 * D0$ , and  $S_1^O = B0 * H0 * L_10 * D0$ . Different from the first BMM, the dimension D in the second BMM can be tiled. When each tile is further split and mapped to a lower-level memory,  $r_2(T^{ra})$  is calculated similarly. We can obtain the maximum value of each tile factor by solving the following inequalities:
> 
> $$r_1(T^{er}) \le S_M, \quad r_2(T^{ra}) \le S_M \tag{1}$$
> 
> where  $S_M$  represents the memory capacity at different levels. Notably, the calculation of  $r_1(T^{er})$  and  $r_2(T^{ra})$  is also applicable to other accelerators such as TPU [13] and Simba [16].
> 
> Intra-operator parallel factors are constrained by the number of compute cores. The required computation capacity  $r_3(P^{ra})$  for two BMMs is formulated as:
> 
> $$r_3(P^{ra}) = P_B * P_H * P_{L_1} * P_{L_2} * P_D$$
> 
> where  $P_B$ ,  $P_H$ ,  $P_{L_1}$ ,  $P_{L_2}$  and  $P_D$  represent the parallel factor of each loop. We can obtain the maximum value of each parallel factor by solving the following inequality:
> 
> $$r_3(P^{ra}) \le P_C$$
> 
> where  $P_C$  represents the number of parallel compute cores.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20195.png)

Mapping后程序的**性能估计**

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ## C. Cost Modeling
> 
> Each tensor is decomposed into multiple tiles, which are temporally mapped onto the memory hierarchy and spatially mapped onto compute cores. Therefore, the latency cycles required for each mapping candidate involve compute latency and memory access latency. The compute latency of each operator is calculated by dividing the number of operations by the number of used compute cores:
> 
> Compute Latency = 
> $$\frac{\text{\# of operations}}{\text{\# of used compute cores}}$$
> 
> The number of used compute cores can be obtained according to intra-operator parallelization. The memory access latency of each operator is the maximum among all memory levels, considering spatial accelerators are designed in hierarchy:
> 
> Memory Latency = 
> $$\max_{m \in M}$$
>  (Memory Latency(m))
> 
> The memory access latency at each level is calculated by dividing the number of memory accesses (i.e., data read-write) by the memory bandwidth:
> 
> Memory Latency
> $$(m) = \frac{\text{\# of Accesses}(m)}{\text{Bandwidth}(m)} \quad m \in [0, M]$$
>   
> 
> $$\text{Accesses}(m) = \text{Reads}(m) + \text{Writes}(m)$$
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20196.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> The amount of data read-write can be calculated according to inter-operator and intra-operator tiling. The latency  $\mathcal{L}$  of each operator is the maximum value between compute latency and memory latency:
> 
> $$\mathcal{L} = max(\text{Compute Latency}, \text{Memory Latency})$$
> 
> Given a mapping, the final latency for multi-head attention is the sum of the latency of each operator. Notably, the number of memory accesses of each operator is different after operator fusion. In the first BMM, there is no need to write tensor A back to L2 memory, as shown in Fig. 4. In the second BMM, there is no need to read tensor A' from L2 memory. Energy is also a performance metric, which is related to the number of memory accesses and computation operations. It is modeled through a weighted sum of the memory accesses across the hierarchy, following prior works [17], [18].
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20197.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ## V. RL-BASED EXPLORATION
> 
> The workflow of RL-based exploration is depicted in Fig. 5(a). The RL agent regards the mapping space from Section IV-B as the action space and continuously optimizes it during exploration. After generating mappings, the agent receives performance metrics through cost modeling. It transforms these metrics into a reward and then optimizes its policy to maximize performance.
> 
> In the reinforcement learning formulation, there are four
> 
> main components: a state, an action space, a reward function, and a learning algorithm. Given a state  $s_t$  at time step t, the agent takes an action  $a_t$  from the action space  $A_t$  according to its policy  $\pi$ . In return, the agent receives the next state  $s_{t+1}$  and a scalar reward  $r_t$ . The reward is calculated through the reward function, and the policy is optimized by the learning algorithm. We define each component as follows.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20199.png)

## 实验

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ## VI. EVALUATION
> 
> ## A. Experimental Setup
> 
> For multi-head attention benchmarks, we use **BERT** [2], T5 [3], GPT [4], and GLM [5]. We implement four mapping approaches for performance comparison: CoSA [15], Ansor [10], FlashAttention [7], and FLAT [12]. For spatial accelerators, we use NVDLA [9] and TPU [13]. NVDLA has 512KB shared memory, 8KB register and 1024 processing elements (PEs). TPU has 28MB on-chip memory and 65536 PEs. We first show the performance results of transformer models and report geometric mean speedups to the baselines. Then, we show the energy results and exploration efficiency of TileMap. Finally, we discuss TileMap's sensitivity to batch size and sequence length.

On NVDLA, TileMap achieves  $3.1\times$ ,  $2.4\times$ ,  $2.3\times$ , and  $2.0\times$  speedup on average, relative to CoSA, Ansor, FlashAttention, and FLAT respectively. On TPU, TileMap achieves  $2.6 \times$ ,  $1.9 \times$ ,  $2.1 \times$ , and  $1.8 \times$  speedup on average, relative to CoSA, Ansor, FlashAttention, and FLAT respectively. CoSA maps each operator on accelerators at a time without fusion. It leads to poorer performance and compute utilization due to significant off-chip memory access

## idea

Q：HLS实现出高性能的RTL代码（数据流加速器），**调度**决定生成RTL的性能；

数据依赖和资源依赖决定**算子**顺序;

在DFG中引入**资源约束**，构建ODG；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> <mark>of operations</mark> in program. The order is related to operation dependencies, including data and resource dependencies. Data dependencies are intrinsic in programs, while resource dependencies are determined by scheduling methods. Existing scheduling methods lack an accurate and complete operation dependency graph (ODG), leading to poor performance. In this paper, we propose an ODG-based scheduling method for HLS with GNN and RL. We adopt GNN to perceive accurate relations between operations. We use the relations to guide an RL agent in building a complete ODG. We perform feedback-guided iterative scheduling with the graph to converge to a high-quality solution. Experiments show that our method reduces 23.8% and 16.4% latency on average, compared with the latest GNN-based and RL-based methods, respectively.
> 
> Abstract—Scheduling determines the execution order and time
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20210.png)

问题：；

启发式方法

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> Existing heuristic methods adopt handcrafted policies to sequentially build the ODG following topological order. As soon/late as possible (ASAP/ALAP) method [3] sequentially schedules operations to their earliest/latest available clock cycles (CLC) regardless of resource dependencies. List scheduling (LS) [4] considers resource dependencies. Resource dependencies are inserted when the number of scheduled operations
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20211.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> in the current CLC reaches the resource constraints. These dependencies are from the currently scheduled operation to the following operations in the topological order. Inspired by LS, entropy-directed scheduling [5] sequentially inserts resource dependencies and schedules operations to obtain maximum entropies. These methods simplify the insertion of resource dependencies, resulting in an incomplete ODG.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20212.png)

LP、ILP、GNN

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ODG. Integer LP (ILP) method [6] formalizes the LP problem with integer constraints. The method adopts a branch-andbound method to search for complete and accurate resource dependencies within the entire feasible space. The search method follows a trial-and-error strategy, without the help of dependency information from the program. Therefore, ILP method suffers from weak scalability when facing diverse structured DFGs and many branches. System of difference constraints (SDC) method [7] improves scalability by a heuristic transformation scheme. The transformation introduces suboptimal resource dependencies, resulting in inaccurate ODG.
> 
> Existing linear programming (LP)-based methods formalize
> 
> the scheduling problem as LP problem to build a complete
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20213.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> are designed for scheduling. GNN-based method improves the LS method by customizing scheduling priority for DFG, reducing latency. It still uses a heuristic policy to insert resource dependency, resulting in an incomplete ODG. RLbased method iteratively improves scheduling solution guided by the perceived resource requirements among CLCs. This method can hardly learn operation relations, degrading ODG accuracy. Observing these disadvantages, we integrate GNN with RL to build a complete and accurate ODG for efficient scheduling. Our basic idea is leveraging GNN to perceive resource requirements among operations. This scheme can express accurate relations among operations. The accurate relations can guide an RL agent to build a complete ODG.
> 
> Recently, GNN-based method [8] and RL-based method [9]
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20214.png)

## 方法

GNN+RL

> Fig. 1. The overview of our proposed feedback guided iterative scheduling with ODG. Applying the example action  $o_4^+$  inserts resource dependency  $o_3 \rightarrow o_4$  for updating ODG, and reschedules  $o_4$  to its next CLC  $c_4$ .
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20215.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ization is conducted by ASAP/ALAP [3] method with given input DFG, device characteristics, and constraints. The ASAP schedule is adopted as the initial schedule. 2 The input, initial, and current schedules are analyzed to conduct characteristicbased node encoding, generating a state. 3 The state is fed into a GNN-based policy network to conduct operation relation perception, generating a policy with normalized mean and variance of a Gaussian distribution. (4) The normalized Gaussian distribution is scaled and mapped into a legal action space through legalization. The legal action space comprises candidate actions satisfying pre-defined constraints. Afterwards, the legalized Gaussian distribution is sampled to generate action. (5) RL agent takes the action to insert resource dependencies for the chosen operation, updating ODG. 6 The dependency insertion modifies the execution order and time for operations, rescheduling the chosen operation to its previous/next CLC
> 
> and generating the next schedule.
> 
> Specifically, our method has eight steps. ① The initial-
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20216.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> a high-quality scheduling solution. The iteration stops when the maximum timesteps are reached, or the schedule does not improve for several timesteps (early stop). 7 During the training process, the improved schedule is compared with its previous one to calculate the feedback reward. The feedback reward evaluates resource usage and latency reductions of the improved schedule, as well as constraint violation of the
> 
> In specific RL episode, 2–6 are iterated to converge to
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20217.png)

> action. (8) The feedback reward is recorded and accumulated in each episode. After several episodes, the cumulative feedback rewards are used to update the parameters of our GNNbased policy network through gradient ascent [13]. This update guides the training of GNN and RL for complete and accurate ODGs and high-quality scheduling solutions.

GNN**感知**运算的关系

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ![](_page_0_Figure_0.jpeg)
> 
> Fig. 3. The operation relation perception with GNN. The values in the braces show the transformation of hiddensizes for single embedding. Multi-layer perceptron (MLP) consists of multiple fully connected (FC) layers [14].
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20222.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> Our GNN-based policy network perceives operation relations to generate a Gaussian distribution. We generate action with the distribution for dependency insertion and ODG updating. Fig. 3 shows the three phases of our policy network. (1) The bi-directional relation perception phase comprises four bi-directional perception diffusion modules. The last diffusion module (4th module) is connected with others through
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20223.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> By serially applying these diffusion modules, node embedding iteratively passes to their higher-order adjacencies. Moreover, this serial application increases the node embedding dimension from 7 to 32 to 64. This iterative embedding passing and dimension increase enables the perception of operation relations with higher scope ranges. Afterwards, these perceptions of different scopes are fused by a perception fusion scheme in the last diffusion module (4th module). The scheme concatenates the state, updated embeddings in modules 1–3, and the aggregated embedding of both directions in module 4. In subsequent embedding update, these perceptions are fused, enhancing the updating of the ODG. Moreover, the scheme alleviates the vanishing gradient and over-smoothing phenomena, facilitating the model training [15].
> 
> (2) In the global perception aggregation phase, we aggregate all node embeddings to generate a graph embedding. This aggregation enables a comprehensive expression of operation relations. We first use FC Attention to generate the attention value vector [16] for updated node embedding matrix 4. These attention values weight their corresponding node embeddings with importance. By conducting Add Pooling, these weighted embeddings are summed to generate graph embedding [14].
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20224.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ![](_page_0_Figure_0.jpeg)
> 
> Fig. 4. The bi-directional perception diffusion and fusion schemes. The red circle and boxes illustrate the forward and backward aggregations for  $o_2$ .
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20225.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> a perception fusion scheme. Fig. 4 shows that each diffusion module comprises a forward and a backward aggregation layer, as well as an FC layer [14]. Rather than the vanilla module without backward aggregation, the adopted bi-directional aggregation enhances the expression of operation relations in both directions. These aggregation layers update each node embedding vector by aggregating the embeddings of its predecessors/successors, respectively. For instance, in forward aggregation, the embedding of  $o_2$  aggregates the embedding of  $o_1$ to generate its forward aggregated embedding. Alternatively, the backward one is generated by aggregating the embedding of  $o_4$  and  $o_6$  in backward aggregation. After aggregations, the two versions of aggregated embedding matrices are concatenated, and transformed to the desired output size by the FC layer. The transformed embedding matrix is activated by the LeakyReLU function [14] to generate the updated node embedding matrix for this diffusion module.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20226.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> (3) In the Gaussian distribution generating phase, we use the graph embedding to generate normalized mean  $\mu_n$  and variance  $\sigma^2$  of a Gaussian distribution [17]. Notably, we adopt the Gaussian distribution to construct continuous action space for adapting to DFGs with variable operation quantities. We first transform the 64-dimensional graph embedding into two real values by MLP [14] Mean and MLP Variance, respectively. Then, we activate these values using Tanh and Softplus functions [14], respectively. The Tanh function contributes to a symmetrical normalized mean value space of [-1, 1], which is used to construct the action distributions in two opposite directions. The Softplus function contributes to nonnegative variance for introducing randomness. Afterwards, the generated Gaussian distribution is used in the ODG update and iterative schedule.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20227.png)

RL迭代schedule并**更新ODG**

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> Fig. 5 gives an example of resource dependency insertion and rescheduling with RL in ODG update. ① The state in Fig. 2 is inferred by the policy network, generating a normalized Gaussian distribution  $\mathcal{N}(0.5, 2)$ . ② The agent analyzes data dependencies and the current schedule, determining  $\mathcal{A}_{\text{legal}} = \{o_4, o_6\}$ . Specifically,  $o_1$  cannot be rescheduled to the previous CLC as it is already in the first CLC  $c_1$ . Moreover,  $o_1$  cannot be rescheduled to the next CLC  $c_2$ , which is already occupied by its successors  $o_2$  and  $o_3$ . Similarly,  $o_2$ ,  $o_3$ , and  $o_5$  are also illegal due to their dependencies. Therefore, only operations  $o_4$  and  $o_6$  can be rescheduled to their next CLCs, respectively. 3 The normalized Gaussian distribution  $\mathcal{N}(0.5,2)$  is legalized to  $\mathcal{N}(1,2)$  by  $|\mathcal{A}_{legal}|=2$ . (4) A real value  $i_t = 1.2$  is sampled from  $\mathcal{N}(1,2)$ . ⑤ The index  $i_{t,r}$ of  $\mathcal{A}_{legal}$  is calculated as min(2, round(|1.2 - 1|) + 1) = 1. Therefore, the 1st operation  $o_4$  in  $\mathcal{A}_{legal}$  is chosen. Moreover, as 1.2 > 1 (i.e.,  $i_t > \mu$ ), the action  $a_t = o_4^+$ . As depicted in Fig. 2, the starting CLC of  $o_4$  is  $c_3$ , and  $o_3$  shares  $c_3$  with  $o_4$ . Therefore, a resource dependency  $o_3 \rightarrow o_4$  is inserted by applying  $o_4^+$ . Consequently,  $o_4$  is scheduled to its next CLC, i.e.,  $c_5$ , since only  $o_6$  is scheduled in  $c_5$ , and there is no dependency from  $o_6$  to  $o_4$ .
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20228.png)

## basic

HLS代码**Mapping**到实现：Datapath、Ctrl、Memory、IO；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> 求。生成的架构包含数据路径、控制逻辑、存储器接口以及 RTL 与外部世界的通信方式。数据路径由一组存储元素 (例如,寄存器、寄存器文件或存储器)、一组<mark>功能单元</mark>(例如,ALU、乘法器、移位器和其他定制功能)以及<mark>互连</mark>元 素(例如,三态驱动器、多路复用器和总线)组成。每个组件的<mark>执行都需耗时</mark>一个或多个时钟周期、可采用<mark>流水打拍</mark>并
> 
> 且可包含输入或输出寄存器。此外,完整数据路径和控制器在多个阶段内均可流水打拍。
> 
> 高层次综合工具用于将<mark>未定时的高层次规范转变为完全定时的实现</mark>。在此变换期间,会通过实现定制架构来满足规范要
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20202.png)

HLS > **compile > schedule > bind > ctrl > IO > RTL**；

**schedule**：分析运算的**数据依赖和资源依赖**，调度运算完成kernel定义的功能，给出**kernel延迟**；

数据依赖是kernel原生的运算**顺序**，资源依赖是运算分配**空闲**资源或**共享**资源；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> - 对编写的算法进行编译以满足规范:该步骤包含多项代码优化,例如,死码消除、常量折叠、报告不受支持的构造等。
> - 2. <mark>调度</mark>给定时钟周期的运算:
>   - a. "Schedule"(调度)阶段用于基于下列条件来<mark>判定每个时钟周期内发生的运算</mark>:
>     - · 运算的<mark>依赖关系</mark>何时已得到满足或者变为可用。
>     - · 时钟周期长度或<mark>时钟频率</mark>。
>     - · <mark>运算完成所需时间</mark>(由目标器件来定义)。时钟周期越长,单个时钟周期内可完成的运算越多。部分运算 可能需要作为多周期资源来实现。HLS 会将运算自动调度到多个时钟周期内
>     - · 可用<mark>资源</mark>。
>     - · 整合任意用户指定的<mark>优化指令</mark>。
>   - b. 在"Schedule"阶段内,本工具会判定将在<mark>给定周期内执行的运算符以及所需组件的数量</mark>。下一步是判定运算 与对应资源之间的绑定。
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20232.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> - 将运算绑定到功能组件,将变量绑定到存储元素
> - a. 绑定任务会<mark>分配硬件资源</mark>用于实现调度的每项运算,并将运算符(例如,加法、乘法和移位)映射到特定 RTL 实现。例如,mult 运算可在 RTL 内作为组合乘法器或流水打拍乘法器来实现。
> 
> - c. 如果多项运算使用相同资源,那么此步骤可执行<mark>资源共享</mark>,前提是不在同一个周期内使用。
> - 4. <mark>控制逻辑</mark>抽取可创建有限状态机 (FSM),根据定义的调度按顺序执行 RTL 设计中的运算。
> 
> b. 绑定任务会将<mark>存储器和/或寄存器分配</mark>到函数内的阵列变量以满足目标性能。
> 
> - 5. 创建逻辑以与外部世界进行<mark>通信</mark>:生成的 RTL 将与外部世界进行通信,例如,对来自外部端口或起始/停止逻辑的 数据进行串流,或者访问外部存储器。
> - 6. 最后,生成 RTL 架构

使用AXI**突发**传输的优化LD/ST

**流水线突发**：ReadReq、WriteReq、WriteResp等命令传输**不需要等待**数据传输，而**各自连续传输**；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ## 流水线突发
> 
> 流水线突发通过在单个请求内读取或写入大量数据或最大量的数据来改善函数吞吐量。流水线突发的优势在于<mark>后续请求 (i) 无需等待当前请求 (i) 完成,因为读取请求、写入请求和写入响应均位于循环主体外部</mark>,并且流水线突发可以尽快 执行请求,如以下代码示例所示。这样可以显著提升函数吞吐量,因为读/写整个循环边界所需的时间更短。
> 
> ```
> rb = ReadReq(i, size);
> wb = WriteReq(i, size);
> for(size_t i = 0; i < size; i++) {
>    Write(wb, i) = f(Read(rb, i));
> }
> WriteResp(wb);</pre>
> ```
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20235.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ![](_page_0_Figure_0.jpeg)
> 
> 如果编译器可成功减少来自归纳变量 (i) 和循环次数 (size) 的突发长度,那么它将推断一个大型流水线突发,并且将 ReadReq、WriteReq 和 WriteResp 调用移至循环外部,如"流水线突发"代码示例所示。因此,所有循环迭代的 读取请求都会组合为单个读取请求,所有写入请求也都会组合为单个写入请求。所有读取请求通常会立即发出,而写入请求仅在数据变为可用后才会发出。
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20236.png)

**顺序突发**：ReadReq、WriteReq、WriteResp等命令传输**需要等待**数据传输；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ## 顺序突发
> 
> 顺序突发由大小较小的数据组成,其中<mark>读取请求、写入请求和写入响应均位于循环主体内部</mark>,如以下代码示例中所示。
> 
> ```
> for(size_t i = 0; i < size; i++) {
>     rb = ReadReq(i, 1);
>     wb = WriteReq(i, 1);
>     Write(wb, i) = f(Read(rb, i));
>     WriteResp(wb);
> }</pre>
> ```
> 
> 顺序突发的缺点在于<mark>后续请求 (i+1) 依赖于先前请求 (i) 完成后才能发出,因为它会等待读取请求、写入请求和写入响应</mark> 完成。 这将导致请求之间出现间隔,如下图所示。
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20237.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ![](_page_0_Figure_0.jpeg)
> 
> <mark>顺序突发效率不及流水线突发</mark>,因为它<mark>多次读取或写入少量数据</mark>,以补偿循环边界。虽然这将显著影响吞吐量,但是顺 序突发仍好于无突发。如果您的代码未遵循 突发传输的前置条件和限制,那么 Vitis HLS 使用此突发方法。
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20238.png)

## My Work

加速器都用**tensor**,因为这个抽象灵活而通用,那么优化的是张量的尺寸和执行流(数据流);

多数据流的抽象？硬件重排的抽象？

本质上重排是数据在存储层次之间**route**的一种方式；

**重排**能改善Acc对Mapping的约束吗？增加LD/ST类型的语义，或者增加**重排kernel**？

混合精度的数据格式？dense排布的地址计算开销？

多数据流feather、MathModel-Cosa、Transformer-Soter、TVM三个工作应该有联系?

多数据流Acc提出新的模拟器是因为硬件架构不同于TimeLoop的抽象；

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ## V. LAYOUTLOOP
> 
> FEATHER enables (dataflow, layout) co-switching at the
> 
> layer granularity to achieve optimal latency and energy efficiency. However, deciding which (dataflow, layout) to use for FEATHER is not trivial because both dataflow and layout have huge space, e.g.  $10^{36} \times 10^8$  for a single convolution layer (ResNet-50 layer 1)  $[27]^2$ , necessitating systematic exploration. For this aim, we enhance Timeloop [41], a state-of-the-art dataflow search framework with (1) physical storage modeling and (2) systematic layout assessment capabilities, and term it as Layoutloop to distinguish it from native Timeloop. We employ Layoutloop to explore dataflows under various layouts for *FEATHER*, selecting the dataflow-layout pair that minimizes energy delay product for each layer.
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20240.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> ## A. Physical Storage Modeling Layoutloop models physical storage as (num line ×
> 
> line\_size) 2D array with "conflict\_depth" specifying number of lines in each bank with the following reasoning.Bank Organizations: Current storage uses diverse organiza-
> 
> tions, including 2D/3D with various groupings. Managing these disparate physical organizations can be complex. However, as storage is usually accessed line by line (or block), we can abstract different organizations into a logical num\_line × line\_size 2D array. This abstraction allows layout modeling to handle these 2D abstract arrays directly, retaining generality without dealing with specific physical organizations.
> 
> Bank Port Constraints: Storage comes with an inherent limitation of the total number of ports in each bank. Concurrent
![image.png](meeting-25%2010%2017%EF%BC%88%E5%A4%9A%E6%A0%B8%E4%BB%BF%E7%9C%9F%E3%80%813DGS%E3%80%81RL%20schedule%EF%BC%89/image%20241.png)

> **[图片提取文字 (image.png)]:**
> to bank conflicts. Thus, *conflict\_depth* is utilized to denote the total number of lines within a single bank. B. Bank Conflicts Assessment Layoutloop models slowdown by judging whether bank conflicts occur when analyzing data access to the on-chip buffer with a specific layout. A  $\max(N_P/N_L, 1)$  slowdown is introduced if  $N_L$  lines are accessed from a bank with  $N_P$  ports. Finally, we also modify Timeloop's mapper to consider data layout during dataflow search.
> 
> read/write operations exceeding available read/write ports lead
